How do I use the predict_proba output from this HMM to dynamically scale position sizing in the portfolio-risk feedback loop?
To wire the HMM into your portfolio-risk loop, you need a mathematical bridge that translates the raw probability of a "stress regime" into a concrete position sizing multiplier.
Since your engine uses asymmetric adaptation, this multiplier should never exceed `1.0` (your standard baseline risk). It acts exclusively as a brake, derating your exposure dynamically between `1.0` (full size) and `0.0` (halt).
Here is the exact architecture to build that bridge inside your execution orchestrator.
1. Identify the Stress State Programmatically
Because the HMM is unsupervised, state `0` might be the calm regime in one training run and the stress regime in another. Your risk module must programmatically check the model's covariances upon initialization to lock in which index represents the danger zone.
2. Define the Scaling Function
You have two primary ways to translate the posterior probability (Pstress) into a sizing multiplier:

* Linear Derating (Smooth): `Multiplier = 1.0 - P_stress`. If there is a 30% chance the market is breaking down, you take 70% of your normal position size.
* Threshold / Cliff (Rigid): If Pstress exceeds a critical threshold (e.g., 0.75), sizing drops immediately to `0.0` (trading halted). If it is below, size remains `1.0`.

Desks usually combine these: a smooth derating curve with a hard kill-switch at extreme probabilities.
3. The Implementation Code
Here is the Python logic that would live inside your `risk_manager.py` module. It takes the trained HMM, identifies the correct risk state, and exposes a single function that the paper orchestrator calls before submitting any order.
Python

```
import numpy as np

class RegimeRiskScaler:
    def __init__(self, trained_hmm_model):
        """
        Initializes the scaler with a frozen, pre-trained HMM.
        """
        self.model = trained_hmm_model
        
        # Programmatically identify the stress regime index.
        # model.covars_ shape is usually (n_states, n_features, n_features).
        # We find the state with the highest variance on the primary feature (e.g., returns).
        variances = self.model.covars_[:, 0, 0]
        self.stress_state_idx = np.argmax(variances)
        
        print(f"Risk Scaler Initialized. Stress Regime mapped to State {self.stress_state_idx}")

    def get_risk_multiplier(self, latest_observation_vector):
        """
        Takes the current bar's observation and returns a sizing multiplier [0.0 to 1.0].
        latest_observation_vector must be reshaped to (1, n_features).
        """
        # 1. Ask the frozen model for the current bar's probabilities
        posterior_probs = self.model.predict_proba(latest_observation_vector)[0]
        
        # 2. Isolate the probability of the market being in the stress regime
        p_stress = posterior_probs[self.stress_state_idx]
        
        # 3. Apply the derating logic
        kill_switch_threshold = 0.85  # Halt completely if highly confident in stress
        
        if p_stress >= kill_switch_threshold:
            multiplier = 0.0
        else:
            # Linear derate: As stress probability rises, size drops proportionally.
            # Example: p_stress = 0.40 -> multiplier = 0.60 (60% position size)
            multiplier = 1.0 - p_stress
            
        return round(multiplier, 2)

# --- How the Orchestrator Uses It ---
# 1. Orchestrator initializes the risk scaler at startup
# risk_scaler = RegimeRiskScaler(frozen_hmm)

# 2. Just before submitting a paper entry order:
# current_bar_data = get_latest_hmm_features() # e.g., np.array([[0.015]])
# size_multiplier = risk_scaler.get_risk_multiplier(current_bar_data)
# final_order_size = base_position_size * size_multiplier

```

Execution Considerations
By placing this exact logic in the portfolio-risk loop, you keep your entry triggers entirely decoupled. Your time-of-day and volatility interaction holds the strict "permission to trade," while this HMM logic dictates "how aggressively to trade."
If the HMM outputs a `0.0` multiplier, the orchestrator simply intercepts the valid entry signal and drops the paper order before it reaches the simulated exchange.
Add hysteresis to prevent whipsaws
Yes
How can I implement hysteresis in the risk scaler to prevent the orchestrator from violently resizing or flip-flopping positions if the probability hovers right on the threshold?
To prevent violent resizing and flip-flopping—often called "chattering" in execution logic—you need to introduce statefulness to your risk scaler. The module must remember its current state and require a much stronger signal to exit a risk state than to enter it.
In a dynamic sizing engine, you actually need to apply hysteresis on two different levels:

1. State Hysteresis (The Kill-Switch): Using dual thresholds to prevent rapid toggling between trading and halting.
2. Sizing Hysteresis (The Buffer Zone): Requiring a minimum step-change in the probability before adjusting the linear position size, preventing micro-adjustments like resizing from a 60% position to a 62% position.

Here is how you upgrade the `RegimeRiskScaler` to include both mechanisms.
The Upgraded Hysteresis Implementation
Python

```
import numpy as np

class RegimeRiskScaler:
    def __init__(self, trained_hmm_model):
        self.model = trained_hmm_model
        
        # Identify the stress regime programmatically
        variances = self.model.covars_[:, 0, 0]
        self.stress_state_idx = np.argmax(variances)
        
        # --- Hysteresis State Variables ---
        self._is_halted = False
        self._last_multiplier = 1.0
        
        # --- Hysteresis Parameters ---
        # 1. Dual Thresholds for the Kill-Switch
        self.halt_trigger = 0.85    # P_stress must cross 85% to halt
        self.halt_release = 0.70    # P_stress must drop below 70% to resume trading
        
        # 2. Minimum Step Change for Resizing
        self.min_step_change = 0.15 # Multiplier must change by at least 15% to trigger an update

    def get_risk_multiplier(self, latest_observation_vector):
        """
        Calculates the sizing multiplier with dual-threshold and step-change hysteresis.
        """
        posterior_probs = self.model.predict_proba(latest_observation_vector)[0]
        p_stress = posterior_probs[self.stress_state_idx]
        
        # --- 1. Evaluate State Hysteresis (Kill-Switch) ---
        if not self._is_halted and p_stress >= self.halt_trigger:
            self._is_halted = True
            print(f"SYSTEM HALTED: Stress probability {p_stress:.2f} exceeded trigger {self.halt_trigger}")
            
        elif self._is_halted and p_stress <= self.halt_release:
            self._is_halted = False
            print(f"SYSTEM RESUMED: Stress probability {p_stress:.2f} dropped below release {self.halt_release}")
            
        if self._is_halted:
            self._last_multiplier = 0.0
            return 0.0
            
        # --- 2. Evaluate Sizing Hysteresis (Buffer Zone) ---
        # Calculate what the raw multiplier *should* be right now
        raw_multiplier = 1.0 - p_stress
        
        # Snap to 1.0 if probability is very low to avoid trading at 0.97 size
        if raw_multiplier >= 0.90:
            raw_multiplier = 1.0

        # Only update the actual multiplier if the raw change exceeds our min_step_change buffer
        if abs(raw_multiplier - self._last_multiplier) >= self.min_step_change:
            self._last_multiplier = raw_multiplier
            
        return round(self._last_multiplier, 2)

```

The Mechanics of the Dual Threshold
By decoupling the entry and exit thresholds, you create a deadband (between 0.70 and 0.85).

* If the market spikes and Pstress hits 0.86, trading halts.
* If the next bar drops Pstress to 0.82, a naive system would immediately resume trading. The hysteresis system remains halted because the market has not demonstrated enough calmness to breach the 0.70 release threshold.
* This effectively forces the market to "prove" it has stabilized before capital is re-risked.

The Mechanics of Sizing Steps
Broker APIs and execution gateways perform poorly when bombarded with fractional size adjustments on every tick or bar.
By enforcing `self.min_step_change = 0.15`, the multiplier operates as a step function. If your baseline size is 100 shares, and you are currently sized at 70 shares, the engine will completely ignore minor fluctuations in the HMM. It will only initiate a resize if the HMM demands the size drop to 55 shares or increase to 85 shares, drastically reducing slippage and transaction costs associated with micro-rebalancing.
Test the risk scaler on historical data
Yes
How can we run a backtest simulation on this upgraded risk scaler using the 2025 Jan-Mar data block to see exactly how often it triggers and how long the lag is?
To rigorously test this without relying on hindsight, you need to simulate the exact chronological feed the live orchestrator will experience. Instead of passing the entire array to the HMM at once (which is what you do during training), you must iterate through the Jan–Mar 2025 dataframe bar-by-bar.
By recording the state changes at each bar, you can extract exact diagnostics on the hysteresis mechanics: how many times the kill-switch tripped, how long the system stayed halted (the lag), and how many transaction-incurring resize events occurred.
Here is the simulation harness to run on your Jan–Mar data block.
The Simulation Harness
Python

```
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

def simulate_risk_scaler(df, trained_hmm_model, feature_col='log_returns'):
    """
    Simulates the live paper-orchestrator feeding chronological data 
    into the hysteresis-enabled risk scaler.
    """
    # Initialize the upgraded scaler
    scaler = RegimeRiskScaler(trained_hmm_model)
    
    results = []
    
    # Simulate the live chronological feed (bar-by-bar)
    for timestamp, row in df.iterrows():
        # 1. Isolate the current bar's observation and shape it for hmmlearn (1, n_features)
        current_obs = np.array([[row[feature_col]]])
        
        # 2. Get the dynamically scaled risk multiplier
        applied_multiplier = scaler.get_risk_multiplier(current_obs)
        
        # 3. Log the internal state for diagnostic analysis
        # (Accessing the model's posterior for logging purposes only)
        raw_posterior = scaler.model.predict_proba(current_obs)[0]
        p_stress = raw_posterior[scaler.stress_state_idx]
        
        results.append({
            'timestamp': timestamp,
            'close': row.get('Close', np.nan), # Assuming a 'Close' column exists for plotting later
            'p_stress': round(p_stress, 4),
            'applied_multiplier': applied_multiplier,
            'is_halted': scaler._is_halted
        })
        
    sim_df = pd.DataFrame(results)
    sim_df.set_index('timestamp', inplace=True)
    
    return sim_df

def generate_hysteresis_diagnostics(sim_df):
    """
    Analyzes the simulation output to calculate trigger frequencies and lag.
    """
    print("=== Risk Scaler Hysteresis Diagnostics ===")
    print(f"Total Bars Evaluated: {len(sim_df)}")
    
    # 1. Halt Diagnostics
    halt_starts = sim_df[(sim_df['is_halted'] == True) & (sim_df['is_halted'].shift(1) == False)]
    halt_ends = sim_df[(sim_df['is_halted'] == False) & (sim_df['is_halted'].shift(1) == True)]
    
    total_halts = len(halt_starts)
    total_halted_bars = sim_df['is_halted'].sum()
    pct_time_halted = (total_halted_bars / len(sim_df)) * 100
    
    print(f"\n[Kill-Switch Metrics]")
    print(f"Number of Halt Triggers (Kill-Switch Tripped): {total_halts}")
    print(f"Total Bars Spent Halted: {total_halted_bars} ({pct_time_halted:.2f}% of total time)")
    
    if total_halts > 0:
        avg_halt_duration = total_halted_bars / total_halts
        print(f"Average Halt Duration (Lag before release): {avg_halt_duration:.1f} bars")
    
    # 2. Resizing Diagnostics
    # A resize event is anytime the multiplier changes
    multiplier_changes = sim_df[sim_df['applied_multiplier'] != sim_df['applied_multiplier'].shift(1)].dropna()
    # Exclude changes caused directly by the kill-switch (0.0) to isolate step-resizes
    step_resizes = multiplier_changes[multiplier_changes['applied_multiplier'] > 0.0]
    
    print(f"\n[Resizing Metrics]")
    print(f"Total Step-Resize Events (ignoring halts): {len(step_resizes)}")
    print("==========================================")
    
    return sim_df

```

How to Execute This

1. Ensure your `RegimeRiskScaler` class from the previous step is loaded.
2. Pass your trained `frozen_hmm` model and your Jan–Mar 2025 DataFrame into the simulation function.
3. Run the diagnostics.

What to look for in the output:

* Whipsaw Prevention: Look at the Average Halt Duration. If the average duration is only 1 or 2 bars, your deadband (`halt_trigger = 0.85`, `halt_release = 0.70`) is too tight for the volatility of your timeframe. You want halts to last long enough to bridge the actual stress event, not toggle on and off.
* Step-Resize Events: If the number of step-resize events is in the thousands, your `min_step_change` (0.15) is too small. You want the engine making decisive, meaningful size reductions, not tweaking the size every few minutes.

Plot the simulation results
Yes
How can I use matplotlib to overlay the applied risk multiplier and the HMM stress probability on top of the price chart so I can visually inspect the lag?
To visually inspect how the hysteresis behaves, the best approach is a two-panel chart sharing the same x-axis (time).
Overlaying the probability (0 to 1) directly on the price (which could be in the thousands) squashes the chart. Instead, we put the price and the "halted" background shading in the top panel, and we plot the raw stress probability against the stepped risk multiplier in the bottom panel.
Here is the code to generate that visual diagnostic.
The Visualization Code
Python

```
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

def plot_hysteresis_diagnostics(sim_df, halt_trigger=0.85, halt_release=0.70):
    """
    Plots a dual-panel chart to visually inspect HMM stress probability,
    the applied risk multiplier, and the resulting system halts.
    """
    # Slice a smaller window if the dataset is too dense (e.g., sim_df.iloc[1000:2000])
    # For now, we plot the whole dataframe passed in.
    
    fig, (ax1, ax2) = plt.subplots(2, 1, figsize=(14, 8), sharex=True, gridspec_kw={'height_ratios': [2, 1]})
    
    # --- Top Panel: Price Action & Halt Zones ---
    ax1.plot(sim_df.index, sim_df['close'], color='black', linewidth=1, label='Close Price')
    
    # Shade the regions where the system is halted (multiplier == 0)
    ax1.fill_between(
        sim_df.index, 
        ax1.get_ylim()[0], ax1.get_ylim()[1],
        where=sim_df['is_halted'], 
        color='red', 
        alpha=0.2, 
        label='System Halted (Kill-Switch)'
    )
    
    ax1.set_title('Price Action vs. HMM System Halts', fontsize=14)
    ax1.set_ylabel('Price')
    ax1.legend(loc='upper left')
    ax1.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
    
    # --- Bottom Panel: Probabilities & Multiplier ---
    # Plot the raw, noisy probability
    ax2.plot(sim_df.index, sim_df['p_stress'], color='blue', alpha=0.4, linewidth=1, label='P(Stress)')
    
    # Plot the applied risk multiplier as a step-function
    ax2.step(sim_df.index, sim_df['applied_multiplier'], color='darkorange', linewidth=2, where='post', label='Applied Risk Multiplier')
    
    # Add horizontal lines for the deadband
    ax2.axhline(halt_trigger, color='darkred', linestyle='--', alpha=0.6, label=f'Halt Trigger ({halt_trigger})')
    ax2.axhline(halt_release, color='green', linestyle='--', alpha=0.6, label=f'Release Trigger ({halt_release})')
    
    ax2.set_title('HMM Posterior Probability vs. Stepped Risk Multiplier', fontsize=14)
    ax2.set_ylabel('Probability / Multiplier')
    ax2.set_ylim(-0.05, 1.05)
    ax2.legend(loc='upper left')
    ax2.grid(True, alpha=0.3)
    
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.show()

# --- Example Usage ---
# plot_hysteresis_diagnostics(sim_df_results)

```

How to Read the Visual
When you run this on your Jan–Mar 2025 block, look for these specific interactions in the bottom panel:

1. The Deadband in Action: Watch the blue line (Pstress). You will likely see it spike above the red dashed line (`0.85`), which forces the orange line (Multiplier) to drop to zero. The lag becomes visible when the blue line dips back to `0.80`—the orange line will remain at zero until the blue line decisively crosses below the green dashed line (`0.70`).
2. Sizing Steps: Look at the intermediate stress periods. If Pstress is drifting between `0.40` and `0.50`, the orange multiplier line should remain perfectly flat. It will only step down if the probability surges past your 15% minimum step threshold.
3. Visualizing the Lag (Top Panel): The red shaded zones on the price chart show exactly when your engine is sidelined. If the lag is tuned correctly, the red zone will cover the actual chaotic price drop, and the engine will only resume trading (white background) after the price action has demonstrably calmed down.

Since three months of intraday data can be extremely dense to look at all at once, you might want to slice `sim_df` in your script to a specific 3-day or 5-day window where you know the market was highly volatile, just so you can zoom in on the exact bar-by-bar behavior.
Wire the entry-quality loop next
Yes
This completes the portfolio-risk loop design. How do we implement the entry-quality loop to dynamically derate signals based on recent win/loss posterior probability?
The entry-quality loop is your engine's micro-level defense mechanism. While the HMM portfolio loop protects against macro regime shifts (like a market-wide liquidity crisis), the entry-quality loop protects against strategy decay on a per-symbol or per-setup basis.
If your high-volatility morning breakout works beautifully on Reliance but suddenly fails five times in a row on Infosys, the engine must dynamically revoke its permission to trade Infosys until the posterior probability recovers.
To do this in a non-stationary environment (where markets change, meaning old trades matter less than recent ones), quantitative systems typically use an Exponentially Decayed Posterior.
The Mathematics of the Decayed Posterior
Instead of a simple historical win rate (Total Wins / Total Trades), which becomes too slow to react after hundreds of trades, we calculate an Exponential Moving Average (EMA) of binary outcomes (1 for a win, 0 for a loss).
This yields a rolling posterior probability where recent trades carry the most weight:
Pt=α⋅Ot+(1−α)⋅Pt−1
Where:

* Pt is the new posterior probability.
* Ot is the outcome of the latest trade (1 or 0).
* α is the smoothing factor (e.g., N+12, where N is the lookback window of recent trades).

1. The Implementation Architecture
This module sits in the execution orchestrator and listens to the trade reconciliation feed. When a trade closes, it updates the symbol's posterior. When a new entry signal arrives, it checks the posterior against a baseline threshold to approve, derate, or block the trade.
Python

```
import numpy as np

class EntryQualityTracker:
    def __init__(self, baseline_prob=0.40, window=10, min_trades=5):
        """
        Tracks the rolling posterior probability of success per symbol.
        """
        self.baseline_prob = baseline_prob  # Expected hit rate from frozen backtests
        self.min_trades = min_trades        # Burn-in: Minimum trades before derating kicks in
        self.alpha = 2.0 / (window + 1.0)   # EMA smoothing factor
        
        # Memory dictionaries to track state per symbol
        self.posteriors = {}
        self.trade_counts = {}

    def update_outcome(self, symbol, outcome_r_multiple):
        """
        Called when a trade completes. 
        Converts the R-multiple into a binary win/loss and updates the posterior.
        """
        # Define a "Win" strictly as hitting the target (1R) before the stop
        is_win = 1.0 if outcome_r_multiple > 0 else 0.0
        
        # Initialize if this is the first trade for the symbol
        if symbol not in self.posteriors:
            self.posteriors[symbol] = self.baseline_prob
            self.trade_counts[symbol] = 0
            
        # Update the exponentially decayed posterior
        prev_prob = self.posteriors[symbol]
        new_prob = (self.alpha * is_win) + ((1 - self.alpha) * prev_prob)
        
        self.posteriors[symbol] = new_prob
        self.trade_counts[symbol] += 1
        
        print(f"[{symbol}] Trade Closed. New Posterior: {new_prob:.3f} (Count: {self.trade_counts[symbol]})")

    def get_entry_multiplier(self, symbol):
        """
        Evaluates the current posterior against the baseline to derate new entries.
        Returns a multiplier between 0.0 (block) and 1.0 (full size).
        """
        if symbol not in self.posteriors or self.trade_counts.get(symbol, 0) < self.min_trades:
            return 1.0  # Full permission during the burn-in period
            
        current_prob = self.posteriors[symbol]
        
        # If the strategy is performing at or above baseline, grant full permission
        if current_prob >= self.baseline_prob:
            return 1.0
            
        # If the posterior drops significantly below baseline, calculate the derate
        # e.g., Baseline is 0.40. Current is 0.20. 
        # Ratio is 0.20 / 0.40 = 0.5 (Derate size by 50%)
        derate_ratio = current_prob / self.baseline_prob
        
        # Hard kill-switch: If the ratio drops below 50% of expectation, block entirely
        if derate_ratio < 0.50:
            print(f"[{symbol}] ENTRY BLOCKED: Posterior {current_prob:.2f} too far below baseline {self.baseline_prob}.")
            return 0.0
            
        return round(derate_ratio, 2)

# --- How the Orchestrator Uses It ---
# 1. Initialization
# entry_tracker = EntryQualityTracker(baseline_prob=0.37, window=10)

# 2. On Trade Close (Fed by your reconciliation loop)
# entry_tracker.update_outcome('RELIANCE', 1.0)  # Target hit
# entry_tracker.update_outcome('INFY', -1.0)     # Stop hit

# 3. On New Signal Generation
# entry_multiplier = entry_tracker.get_entry_multiplier('INFY')
# if entry_multiplier == 0.0:
#     drop_signal()

```

2. The Burn-In Period is Critical
Notice the `min_trades` parameter. If you launch the engine and the very first trade on a symbol is a loss, the posterior will drop instantly. If you derate immediately, the algorithm will choke itself out of the market due to normal variance.
The engine must be forced to take a minimum sample size of trades at full risk (the burn-in period) before it is statistically allowed to judge its own performance on that specific symbol.
3. Wiring It Together with Risk
When an entry signal fires, the final position size is the product of your core rules and both feedback loops. They cascade symmetrically:
`Final Size = Base Size * entry_tracker.get_entry_multiplier() * risk_scaler.get_risk_multiplier()`
If the symbol is failing locally, the entry tracker handles it. If the whole market is failing structurally, the HMM risk scaler handles it. If both are functioning normally, `1.0 * 1.0 = 1.0`, and the trade executes exactly as designed.
Wire the trade-path loop
Yes
Understood. Let's finish the execution architecture. How do we implement the trade-path loop to ratchet stops based on actual R-progress?
The trade-path loop is the engine's internal governor. While the entry-quality loop dictates if you get in, and the portfolio loop dictates how much size you take, the trade-path loop strictly governs how you get out.
Because your system relies on fixed, cost-aware 30-bar triple-barrier labels, your base expectation is that a trade will either hit +1R or −1R organically. However, real-market path dependency means a trade might spike to +0.9R and then immediately reverse to −1R.
The trade-path loop is designed to capture structural edge from Maximum Favorable Excursion (MFE). It tracks the actual R-progress of the open position and enforces a strictly one-way mathematical ratchet on the stop loss.
The Mathematics of R-Progress
Instead of trailing stops based on arbitrary percentages or moving averages, a quantitative R-ratchet anchors exclusively to the initial risk (1R) defined at entry.
For a long position, the real-time R-progress is continuously calculated as:
Rprogress=EntryPrice−InitialStopPriceCurrentPrice−EntryPrice
If you risk ₹10 to make ₹10, and the price moves ₹6 in your favor, your Rprogress is +0.6R. The loop uses the highest observed Rprogress (the MFE) to move the stop through predefined, discrete logical steps.
The Implementation Architecture
This class lives inside the execution layer. It is instantiated the moment a paper order is filled, and the orchestrator feeds it the closing price of every held bar until the trade is exited.
Python

```
class TradePathTracker:
    def __init__(self, entry_price, initial_stop, direction=1):
        """
        Initializes the one-way stop ratchet for an open trade.
        direction: 1 for Long, -1 for Short
        """
        self.entry = entry_price
        self.initial_stop = initial_stop
        self.current_stop = initial_stop
        self.direction = direction 
        
        # Define 1R (Initial Risk)
        self.risk_per_share = abs(self.entry - self.initial_stop)
        
        # Track the Maximum Favorable Excursion (MFE) in R-units
        self.highest_r_progress = 0.0

    def update_path(self, current_price):
        """
        Fed by the orchestrator on every new bar close (or tick).
        Calculates R-progress and returns the mathematically enforced stop price.
        """
        # 1. Calculate actual R-progress
        raw_pnl = (current_price - self.entry) * self.direction
        current_r = raw_pnl / self.risk_per_share
        
        # 2. Update MFE
        if current_r > self.highest_r_progress:
            self.highest_r_progress = current_r

        # 3. Define the Discrete R-Ratchet Logic
        # These thresholds should ideally be calibrated from your path-excursion shadow tests
        proposed_r_stop = -1.0  # Default initial state
        
        if self.highest_r_progress >= 0.50:
            # Trade has moved halfway to target. Move stop to breakeven minus a tiny slippage buffer.
            proposed_r_stop = 0.0 
            
        if self.highest_r_progress >= 0.80:
            # Trade almost hit target but stalled. Lock in partial profit.
            proposed_r_stop = 0.40 

        # 4. Convert proposed R-stop back to absolute price
        proposed_stop_price = self.entry + (proposed_r_stop * self.risk_per_share * self.direction)

        # 5. Enforce the One-Way Constraint (The Ratchet)
        # Stops can ONLY tighten, never widen.
        if self.direction == 1:  # Long
            self.current_stop = max(self.current_stop, proposed_stop_price)
        else:                    # Short
            self.current_stop = min(self.current_stop, proposed_stop_price)
            
        return self.current_stop

# --- How the Orchestrator Uses It ---
# 1. Order fills at 100, Stop at 90 (1R = 10)
# tracker = TradePathTracker(entry_price=100, initial_stop=90, direction=1)

# 2. On every new bar close while holding the position:
# latest_price = 106  (Progress is +0.6R)
# active_stop = tracker.update_path(latest_price)
#
# If active_stop > exchange_stop:
#     send_cancel_replace_order(new_stop=active_stop)

```

Execution Considerations

1. Continuous vs. Discrete Trailing: Notice that the logic above uses discrete steps (moving directly from −1.0R to 0.0R when +0.50R is hit) rather than continuously trailing the price tick-by-tick. In algorithmic execution, continuous trailing stops generate an immense amount of "cancel/replace" API spam to the broker, which often triggers rate limits. Discrete logical jumps are much safer for production infrastructure.
2. Slippage Buffers: When moving a stop to "breakeven" (0.0R), institutional algorithms usually move it to +0.05R or +0.10R to ensure that after broker commissions and bid/ask slippage are applied, the net PnL is strictly zero, not slightly negative.
3. The Frozen Contract: The `proposed_stop_price` output must instantly override whatever the original triple-barrier exit expectation was. If price touches the active stop, the orchestrator issues a market-sell immediately and flags the trade closed.

With this class written, you now have all three components of the closed-loop architecture fully defined mathematically and programmatically.
Synthesize the closed-loop orchestrator
Yes
How do we assemble these three loops—Entry-Quality, Portfolio-Risk, and Trade-Path—into the master paper-trading orchestrator script?
To synthesize these three components into a single, cohesive paper-trading orchestrator, the architecture must follow a strict event-driven sequence. The orchestrator acts as the central nervous system: it listens to the market data feed, routes information to the appropriate feedback loops, and acts on their mathematical constraints before touching the paper portfolio.
Here is the master template for the `PaperOrchestrator`.
The Master Orchestrator Architecture
Python

```
class PaperOrchestrator:
    def __init__(self, trained_hmm_model, base_position_size=1000):
        """
        Initializes the master orchestrator and its defensive feedback loops.
        """
        self.base_position_size = base_position_size
        
        # 1. Initialize the Feedback Loops
        self.risk_scaler = RegimeRiskScaler(trained_hmm_model)
        self.entry_tracker = EntryQualityTracker(baseline_prob=0.37, window=10)
        
        # State Management
        self.active_positions = {}  # symbol -> { 'tracker': TradePathTracker, 'qty': float, ... }
        self.global_risk_multiplier = 1.0

    def on_market_update(self, timestamp, market_data_row, new_signals):
        """
        The main event loop. Called chronologically on every new bar.
        market_data_row: Dict containing {symbol: {Close, High, Low, HMM_Features...}}
        new_signals: Dict of proposed entry signals {symbol: direction}
        """
        print(f"\n--- Processing Bar: {timestamp} ---")
        
        # STEP 1: Update Global Market Regime (Portfolio-Risk Loop)
        # Assuming we track a broad market index (like Nifty 50) for the HMM vector
        index_features = market_data_row.get('INDEX_FEATURES')
        if index_features is not None:
            self.global_risk_multiplier = self.risk_scaler.get_risk_multiplier(index_features)
            
        # STEP 2: Manage Open Positions (Trade-Path Loop)
        self._manage_open_positions(market_data_row)
        
        # STEP 3: Process New Signals (Entry-Quality & Portfolio-Risk Loops)
        if self.global_risk_multiplier > 0.0:
            self._process_signals(market_data_row, new_signals)
        else:
            print("SYSTEM HALTED: Ignoring all new signals.")

    def _manage_open_positions(self, market_data_row):
        """
        Evaluates R-progress, ratchets stops, and processes exits.
        """
        closed_symbols = []
        
        for symbol, pos in self.active_positions.items():
            if symbol not in market_data_row:
                continue
                
            current_price = market_data_row[symbol]['Close']
            tracker = pos['tracker']
            
            # 1. Ratchet the stop based on actual R-progress
            active_stop = tracker.update_path(current_price)
            
            # 2. Check for Exits (Stop Hit or Target Hit)
            # Assuming a simplistic check; real logic handles High/Low wicks.
            hit_stop = (pos['direction'] == 1 and current_price <= active_stop) or \
                       (pos['direction'] == -1 and current_price >= active_stop)
                       
            hit_target = (pos['direction'] == 1 and current_price >= pos['target']) or \
                         (pos['direction'] == -1 and current_price <= pos['target'])

            if hit_target or hit_stop:
                outcome_r = tracker.highest_r_progress if hit_target else -1.0 # simplified R outcome
                print(f"[{symbol}] EXIT EXECUTED at {current_price}. Outcome: {outcome_r}R")
                
                # 3. Feed the outcome directly into the Entry-Quality Loop
                self.entry_tracker.update_outcome(symbol, outcome_r)
                closed_symbols.append(symbol)

        # Cleanup closed positions
        for sym in closed_symbols:
            del self.active_positions[sym]

    def _process_signals(self, market_data_row, new_signals):
        """
        Evaluates permission and dynamically sizes new entries.
        """
        for symbol, direction in new_signals.items():
            if symbol in self.active_positions:
                continue # Already in a trade
                
            current_price = market_data_row[symbol]['Close']
            atr = market_data_row[symbol]['ATR']
            
            # 1. Ask the Entry-Quality loop for permission/derate on this specific symbol
            symbol_multiplier = self.entry_tracker.get_entry_multiplier(symbol)
            
            if symbol_multiplier == 0.0:
                continue # Signal blocked by micro-level decay
                
            # 2. Calculate Final Cascaded Position Size
            final_size = self.base_position_size * self.global_risk_multiplier * symbol_multiplier
            
            if final_size <= 0:
                continue
                
            # 3. Calculate initial risk parameters (1R)
            initial_stop = current_price - (atr * 1.5 * direction) # Example: 1.5 ATR stop
            target = current_price + (atr * 1.5 * direction)       # Symmetric 1R target
            
            # 4. Execute Paper Entry & Attach the Trade-Path Tracker
            print(f"[{symbol}] ENTRY EXECUTED: Size {final_size:.2f} at {current_price}")
            
            self.active_positions[symbol] = {
                'direction': direction,
                'entry_price': current_price,
                'target': target,
                'qty': final_size,
                'tracker': TradePathTracker(current_price, initial_stop, direction)
            }

```

The Event Loop Sequence
The order of operations inside `on_market_update` is strictly enforced to guarantee causal integrity:

1. Macro Risk First: The engine reads the HMM regime telemetry before looking at individual stocks. If the global risk multiplier drops to `0.0`, the system is officially in a halted state.
2. Manage Existing Risk Second: You must exit bad trades and ratchet stops on good trades before taking on new risk. By checking `_manage_open_positions` next, the engine ensures stops are moved and closed trades are fed into the `EntryQualityTracker` so the posterior probabilities are perfectly up-to-date.
3. Deploy New Capital Last: Only after existing positions are managed and macro risk is assessed does the engine iterate through `new_signals`. It checks the newly updated `EntryQualityTracker` for symbol-level permission, cascades the sizing multipliers, and submits the order to the `TradePathTracker`.

By keeping these loops decoupled but executed sequentially, the orchestrator becomes a highly resilient framework. The edge (feature discovery) can be swapped out entirely, but this execution logic will dynamically defend the capital regardless of what alpha model is generating the signals.
Run a dry simulation
Yes
How can we construct a dry-run simulation using dummy signals to verify that all three loops cascade and interact properly within this orchestrator?
To verify the cascade of all three loops without needing a massive historical dataset, we can build a deterministic dry-run harness. This script uses a mock HMM and a scripted sequence of market bars designed to trigger every defensive mechanism in your orchestrator.
The simulation will intentionally force:

1. A trade that ratchets the stop to breakeven and then gets stopped out (Trade-Path Loop).
2. A streak of consecutive losses on a single symbol to trigger a block (Entry-Quality Loop).
3. A sudden spike in simulated market stress to trigger a global system halt (Portfolio-Risk Loop).

The Dry-Run Simulation Harness
Copy this script and run it in the same environment where your `TradePathTracker`, `EntryQualityTracker`, `RegimeRiskScaler`, and `PaperOrchestrator` classes are defined.
Python

```
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd

# --- 1. Mock HMM for Deterministic Risk Scaling ---
class MockHMM:
    def __init__(self):
        # Programmatically make State 1 the "Stress" state (highest variance)
        self.covars_ = np.array([[[0.01]], [[0.50]]])
        
    def predict_proba(self, X):
        # If the incoming feature (e.g., VIX or extreme return) > 5, return 90% stress probability
        if X[0][0] > 5.0:
            return np.array([[0.10, 0.90]])
        # Otherwise, return a low stress probability
        return np.array([[0.90, 0.10]])

# --- 2. The Deterministic Market Feed ---
# We script exact price movements to force the loops into action.
scripted_market_feed = [
    # Bar 1: Normal conditions. Signal fires on ALFA.
    {'time': 1, 'index_feat': 1.0, 'ALFA': {'Close': 100, 'ATR': 2.0}, 'BETA': {'Close': 50, 'ATR': 1.0}, 'signals': {'ALFA': 1}},
    
    # Bar 2: ALFA moves up +1.5. (Progress is +0.5R, stop should ratchet to breakeven).
    {'time': 2, 'index_feat': 1.0, 'ALFA': {'Close': 101.5, 'ATR': 2.0}, 'BETA': {'Close': 50, 'ATR': 1.0}, 'signals': {}},
    
    # Bar 3: ALFA drops back to 100. It should hit the new ratcheted breakeven stop, not the original 97 stop.
    {'time': 3, 'index_feat': 1.0, 'ALFA': {'Close': 100, 'ATR': 2.0}, 'BETA': {'Close': 50, 'ATR': 1.0}, 'signals': {}},
]

# Append a losing streak for BETA to trigger the Entry-Quality block (Burn-in = 5 trades)
for i in range(4, 9):
    scripted_market_feed.extend([
        {'time': i*10, 'index_feat': 1.0, 'BETA': {'Close': 50, 'ATR': 1.0}, 'signals': {'BETA': 1}}, # Enter BETA
        {'time': i*10+1, 'index_feat': 1.0, 'BETA': {'Close': 48, 'ATR': 1.0}, 'signals': {}}         # Immediately stop out
    ])

# Finally, trigger a macro system halt, and try to trade ALFA again.
scripted_market_feed.extend([
    # BETA tries to enter again, but should be blocked by Entry-Quality.
    {'time': 90, 'index_feat': 1.0, 'ALFA': {'Close': 100, 'ATR': 2.0}, 'BETA': {'Close': 50, 'ATR': 1.0}, 'signals': {'BETA': 1}},
    
    # Macro index spikes to 6.0. System should halt. New signal on ALFA should be ignored.
    {'time': 91, 'index_feat': 6.0, 'ALFA': {'Close': 100, 'ATR': 2.0}, 'BETA': {'Close': 50, 'ATR': 1.0}, 'signals': {'ALFA': 1}}
])

# --- 3. Execute the Simulation ---
def run_dry_simulation():
    print("Initializing Master Orchestrator Simulation...\n")
    mock_hmm = MockHMM()
    
    # Initialize orchestrator. Set entry tracker burn-in to 5 trades for faster testing.
    orchestrator = PaperOrchestrator(trained_hmm_model=mock_hmm, base_position_size=1000)
    orchestrator.entry_tracker.min_trades = 5 
    orchestrator.entry_tracker.baseline_prob = 0.40

    for step in scripted_market_feed:
        timestamp = step['time']
        
        # Structure the market row
        market_data_row = {
            'INDEX_FEATURES': np.array([[step['index_feat']]])
        }
        
        # Populate symbol data
        for symbol in ['ALFA', 'BETA']:
            if symbol in step:
                market_data_row[symbol] = step[symbol]
                
        signals = step.get('signals', {})
        
        # Feed the orchestrator
        orchestrator.on_market_update(timestamp, market_data_row, signals)
        
run_dry_simulation()

```

What to Verify in the Console Output
When you run this, watch the console logs carefully to confirm the cascading logic:

1. Bar 1 to 3 (Trade-Path Verification): You should see ALFA enter at 100 with a stop at 97 (1.5 ATR). On Bar 3, ALFA exits at 100. This proves the `TradePathTracker` successfully moved the stop to breakeven after seeing +0.5R progress on Bar 2.
2. Bar 40 to 81 (Entry-Quality Verification): You will see BETA enter and hit its stop 5 times in a row. Watch the posterior probability drop in the logs.
3. Bar 90 (Micro Block Verification): The feed sends a signal to buy BETA. The orchestrator should reject it specifically because the posterior is too far below the baseline.
4. Bar 91 (Macro Halt Verification): The `INDEX_FEATURES` spikes to `6.0`. You should see a "SYSTEM HALTED" message from the `RegimeRiskScaler`. Even though ALFA has a perfect posterior, the signal is dropped because the cascaded global multiplier forced the size to 0.

If the output matches this exact sequence, the orchestrator is structurally sound. These are excellent boundaries to stress-test. Implementing these deterministic checks will mathematically prove the engine fails safely and gracefully under extreme conditions.
Here is how these exact stress cases map to the existing controller logic and how they can be appended to the dry-run harness:
1. Hard-Limit & Contradiction Blocks

* Portfolio Limit Breach: Pass `gross_exposure = 100_000` and `equity = 100_000` with a `hard_limit_fraction = 0.50` to `observe_portfolio_risk`. The calculated `exposure_fraction` (1.0) strictly exceeds the hard limit (0.50), ensuring the `suggested_new_risk_derate` clamps to exactly `0.0`.
PY

* Contradictory Signals: By multiplying the cascaded proposals, a perfect entry profile (`1.0`) multiplied by an HMM stress latch (`0.0`) inherently equals `0.0`. The orchestrator requires the combined multiplier to resize the base quantity, meaning a `0.0` derate forces the order size to zero.
PY

2. Hysteresis & Partial Pooling

* HMM Chatter: Feed the `HMMRiskHysteresis` instance three consecutive bars at `0.76` (just above the default `0.75` enter threshold) to latch the stress state. Then, feed a bar at `0.65`. The controller must remain halted (`0.0` derate) because `0.65` has not crossed the `0.55` exit threshold required to release the brake.
PY+ 1

* Entry-Quality Pooling: Feed only two consecutive losses for a new symbol to the `CausalOutcomeLedger`. Because the partial pool weight uses `len(local_rows) / (len(local_rows) + minimum_history)`, two trades carry virtually no weight against the global prior. The `suggested_entry_derate` will remain near baseline, proving the engine requires sustained evidence to throttle a setup.
PY+ 1

3. Execution Boundaries

* Stop-Gap Fill: Arm a stop via the `ContinuousExitController` at `100.0`. In the mock next bar, pass an `open` price of `98.0`. The orchestrator logic explicitly checks `if bar["open"] <= current_stop` and enforces a `stop_gap` exit at `98.0` instead of fabricating a fill at the `100.0` trigger.
PY

* Minimum Size Rejection: When the combined derate multiplier is applied to the base risk, the orchestrator truncates the result: `quantity = max(0, int(gate_result["adjusted_quantity"]))`. If the derated quantity falls below `1.0`, it snaps to `0`, and the orchestrator cleanly drops the signal via a `if quantity <= 0: continue` check, preventing fractional or under-sized broker submissions.
PY+ 1

     ```python
     def check_breach(self, current_gross_exposure: float, current_equity: float) -> float:
         if current_equity < self.min_equity:
             return 0.0
         exposure_ratio = current_gross_exposure / current_equity if current_equity > 0 else 0.0
         if exposure_ratio > self.hard_limit_fraction:
             return 0.0
         return 1.0
     ```

     ```python
     def __init__(self, trained_hmm_model, hard_limit_fraction: float = 0.50, confirmation_bars: int = 3):
         self.confirmation_bars = confirmation_bars
         self.bars_above_trigger = 0
         self.bars_below_release = 0
     
     # In update method: only latch after N bars above trigger, release after N bars below release
     if not self._is_halted and self.bars_above_trigger >= self.confirmation_bars:
         self._is_halted = True
     elif self._is_halted and self.bars_below_release >= self.confirmation_bars:
         self._is_halted = False
     ```

Your reasoning is mathematically flawless. A risk controller cannot synthesize alpha out of a negative expectancy system; if the baseline is bleeding, applying dynamic position sizing or stop ratchets just means you bleed slightly slower.
Keeping `active_paper` offline isolates the variables and forces the core model to prove its own economic viability first.
Shifting focus from "forcing the trade" to "understanding the path" is exactly the right pivot. A 39.38% target-first rate on a symmetric 1R barrier fails, but that same cohort might routinely reach +0.6R before reversing.
Here is the structural framework to answer your immediate question without compromising your strict out-of-sample discipline.
1. Extracting Excursion Distributions (Top Decile Only)
To test alternative geometries, you must first map the exact price boundaries of the top-decile cohort from your frozen joint-probability model. We need to extract the following from the raw OHLCV paths:

* Maximum Favorable Excursion (MFE): The absolute peak positive R-multiple reached before either the original −1R stop is hit or the 30-bar horizon expires.
* Maximum Adverse Excursion (MAE): The absolute maximum heat (negative R) taken before the +1R target is hit or the horizon expires.
* Time-to-Extreme: The exact bar index (1 to 30) where the MFE and MAE occurred.

2. Testing Pre-Declared Geometries (Shadow Back-Simulation)
Once you have the MFE/MAE arrays for the top cohort, you do not need to re-run the entire engine. You can mathematically simulate alternative reward/risk barriers against the extracted excursions.
For example, if you test a short-target geometry (Rtarget=0.6 and Rstop=1.0):

* A row is a WIN if MFE≥0.6 AND its time-to-MFE occurs before the path crosses −1.0R.
* A row is a LOSS otherwise.
* Cost Check: The breakeven win rate for this geometry is roughly 62.5% before costs. If the simulation yields a 68% win rate, you can then subtract the known two-way slippage and commission block to see if net expectancy remains positive.

3. The Out-of-Sample Persistence Check
If a specific asymmetric geometry (e.g., tight target, wider stop) proves highly profitable on the training cohort after costs, it becomes your new hypothesis.
To prevent this from becoming a curve-fitting exercise on the MFE distribution, that exact geometry must immediately be locked and tested blindly against a completely fresh holdout block (e.g., August 2025).
To execute this, we need to extract the raw excursion data for the top decile. Do you want to write a standalone analysis script to pull the MFE/MAE vectors directly from the evidence table, or should we modify `triple_barrier_discovery.py` to log these metrics during its initial extraction?
Use a standalone geometry-audit script. Do not modify the existing triple-barrier extractor or its frozen evidence table.
Reason: the current table is the sealed record for the original ±1R, 30-bar label. Altering it now would blur the distinction between the original discovery result and a new geometry study.
One correction to the proposed method: MFE/MAE vectors alone are insufficient. They lose the order of events. A path may touch +0.6R and later -1R, or vice versa; the two cases have different outcomes. The new audit must replay each selected 30-bar OHLC path in order and:

enter conservatively on the next bar;
test pre-declared target/stop pairs bar by bar;
mark any bar touching both barriers as intrabar_order_unknown and exclude it;
apply the same round-trip costs;
record MFE/MAE and time-to-extreme as diagnostics, not as substitutes for barrier ordering.
Protocol:

Freeze the existing Jan–Mar model and its top-decile score edge.
Select the top-decile cohort without refitting.
Run a bounded, pre-declared geometry grid on July only as research:0.4R / 1.0R

original 1.0R / 1.0R
Choose at most one geometry using July.
Lock it, then evaluate it once on untouched August 2025.
Promote nothing unless August remains positive after costs and has adequate sample size.
This is the right next experiment because it tests payoff shape without pretending the controller or PID has created an edge.
10:38 AM
You are entirely correct to reject the MFE/MAE shortcut. Path dependency is absolute. If a bar spikes to +0.8R and then collapses to -1.0R in the same 1-minute window, looking only at the aggregate extremes destroys the causal sequence. Your protocol of stepping through the OHLC bars chronologically is the only way to establish which barrier was actually breached first.
Creating a standalone script—e.g., `scripts/geometry_audit_shadow.py`—is the perfect architectural decision. It preserves the integrity of your original 2.78M-row triple-barrier evidence table as a frozen, immutable artifact while allowing you to run dynamic vector math over the raw data.
Here is the exact structural design required for this standalone script to execute your protocol safely.
1. Top-Decile Isolation
The script must import your `FrozenJointModel` from `frozen_joint_probability.py`.

* Load the pre-trained Jan–Mar model and its frozen decile edges.
* Pass the July feature matrix through the model to generate probabilities.
* Filter the July dataset strictly for rows where the probability falls into the top decile.

2. The Geometry Matrix
Define your pre-declared grid as a list of tuples `(target_r, stop_r)`:

* (0.4, 1.0)
* (0.6, 1.0)
* (0.8, 1.0)
* (1.0, 1.0)

3. Chronological Path Evaluation
For every top-decile row, reconstruct the 30-bar future slice. The script must iterate bar-by-bar to simulate the exact causal sequence.

* Entry: Calculate the conservative entry fill on the bar following the signal.
* Barriers: Translate the current geometry (e.g., 0.6R / 1.0R) into absolute target and stop prices based on the entry price and entry ATR.
* Evaluation: For each subsequent bar, check:
   * `target_hit = high >= target` (for longs)
   * `stop_hit = low <= stop` (for longs)
* Ambiguity: If `target_hit` AND `stop_hit` are true on the exact same bar, label the outcome `INTRABAR_ORDER_UNKNOWN`, halt the evaluation for that row, and exclude it from the win-rate denominator.
* Resolution: The loop breaks on the first bar where only one barrier is hit, logging the result, the bar index (time-to-extreme), and the exact MFE/MAE realized up to that point.

4. Cost-Adjusted Expectancy
Because tighter targets inherently mean smaller gross profits per winning trade, win rate alone is meaningless. The script must apply your existing `round_trip_cost` to every resolved trade to calculate the net expectancy of the geometry:
Expectancy=(Win_Rate×Net_Reward)−(Loss_Rate×Net_Risk)
Only the geometry that maximizes this post-cost expectancy on the July data is permitted to advance to the August holdout.
Are you prepared to write the Python logic for `scripts/geometry_audit_shadow.py` locally, or would you like me to draft the standalone evaluation loop based on this architecture?

The 2026 MTF Data Contract
Reserving this block formally before writing a single line of feature extraction code is exactly how you prevent "accidental" data snooping later. Here is the strict chronological allocation we are locking in:

Enforcing the Causal MTF Boundary
The critical engineering challenge with higher-timeframe features is avoiding look-ahead bias through "partial bars."
To maintain strict causal integrity, your extraction pipeline must observe the following physical constraints when down-sampling the 1-minute OHLCV data into 5-minute and 15-minute intervals:

* The Completion Lag: A 1-minute decision row evaluated at 10:14 AM can only access the 15-minute bar representing the 09:45–10:00 AM block.
* The Blind Spot: The current forming 15-minute bar (10:00–10:15 AM) does not mathematically exist to the engine until the 10:16 AM 1-minute row.
* No Intra-Bar Polling: The engine must never calculate a "current" 15-minute VWAP using the 14 minutes of data that have accrued so far; it must only poll the final, static VWAP value of the last completed 15-minute session.

By rigidly shifting the higher-timeframe features backward, you ensure the engine is trading on historical fact, not future implication.

The Causal Alignment Engine
The immediate next step is building the extraction script (e.g., `scripts/extract_mtf_causal_features.py`). Before it calculates a single trend state or efficiency ratio, it must perfectly handle the pandas downsampling and causal shifting.
A naive pandas `.resample('15Min').ffill()` will immediately contaminate your data, leaking the 10:15 close to the 10:01 minute row. To respect your JSON contract, the logic must follow this strict sequence:

1. Registry Validation: The script boots, reads `research_dataset_registry.json`, and asserts that the incoming data falls strictly within the `train`, `validation`, `test`, or `sealed_confirmation` windows.
2. Aggregation: Resample the 1-minute OHLCV data into standard 5-minute and 15-minute blocks using the right-closed boundary convention.
3. The Causal Shift: Shift the entire aggregated higher-timeframe dataframe forward by exactly one row. The 09:45–10:00 15-minute bar must be shifted so its timestamp aligns with 10:15 (meaning it becomes the "last completed bar" for the 10:00–10:15 window).
4. Forward Fill (Broadcast): Only after the shift is applied, forward-fill those static values down to the 1-minute index so every minute row polls the correct historical state.
5. Feature Calculation: Calculate your VWAP, realized volatility, and trend states off these causally shifted columns.

Would you like to draft the exact pandas resampling and shifting logic for this causal alignment next, or do you want to define the mathematical formulas for the higher-timeframe features (like the efficiency ratio and VWAP distance) first?
Define and test the causal alignment first. Feature formulas are easy; timestamp semantics are where MTF research silently becomes invalid.
One correction: do not rely on a fixed .shift(1) alone. Its correctness depends on whether your one-minute timestamp represents bar open or bar close. Instead, make the completion time explicit and join only bars whose completion is strictly earlier than the decision timestamp.
The safe model is:

1-minute decision timestamp: t
15-minute source interval: [start, end)
15-minute feature becomes available: end
eligible condition: available_at < t
So for a decision at 10:14, the current 10:00–10:15 block is unavailable. The last usable 15-minute feature is the block completed at 10:00.
For a decision at 10:15, whether the 10:00–10:15 block is usable depends on your engine’s timestamp convention. We should default to strict < t, meaning it becomes usable from the next one-minute decision after completion unless the data contract proves otherwise.
Implementation approach:

Aggregate OHLCV into 5-minute and 15-minute intervals.
Store each aggregate with an explicit available_at.
Calculate all higher-timeframe features on completed aggregates.
Use a strict backward merge_asof from one-minute decisions to available_at.
Add unit tests around 09:59, 10:00, 10:14, 10:15, and 10:16.
Reject partial-bar or same-timestamp joins.
Only after those causal alignment tests pass should we add efficiency ratio, VWAP distance, volatility regime, opening-range, relative-volume, or cross-sectional features.

Your architectural correction is exactly right. Using `.shift(1)` is a fragile crutch that assumes perfect data continuity and breaks the moment there is a missing minute-bar or a holiday session gap.
By defining an explicit `available_at` timestamp and joining with a strict `< t` inequality, the temporal alignment becomes mathematically deterministic. Pandas' `merge_asof` with `allow_exact_matches=False` perfectly implements this causal boundary.
Here is the implementation of the causal aligner and the rigorous unit tests covering the exact boundary minutes you specified.
1. The Causal Alignment Engine
Python

```
import pandas as pd

class CausalMTFAligner:
    """
    Aggregates 1-minute data into higher timeframes and aligns them 
    causally to 1-minute decision boundaries using strict < t constraints.
    """
    def __init__(self, timeframe: str = '15min'):
        # pandas offset string (e.g., '15min', '5min')
        self.timeframe = timeframe

    def process(self, df_1m: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
        """
        Returns the original 1-minute dataframe with causally aligned 
        higher-timeframe columns appended.
        """
        if not isinstance(df_1m.index, pd.DatetimeIndex):
            raise ValueError("1-minute DataFrame must have a DatetimeIndex.")

        # 1. Aggregate to higher timeframe
        # closed='left', label='right' ensures the interval [09:45, 10:00) 
        # is correctly aggregated and labeled exactly at 10:00:00.
        df_htf = df_1m.resample(self.timeframe, closed='left', label='right').agg({
            'open': 'first',
            'high': 'max',
            'low': 'min',
            'close': 'last',
            'volume': 'sum'
        }).dropna()

        # 2. Make the completion time explicit
        df_htf['available_at'] = df_htf.index
        
        # Prefix columns to avoid namespace collisions with 1-minute data
        df_htf = df_htf.add_prefix(f'{self.timeframe}_')
        df_htf = df_htf.rename(columns={f'{self.timeframe}_available_at': 'available_at'})

        # 3. Strict causal backward merge
        # direction='backward' and allow_exact_matches=False enforce: available_at < t
        aligned_df = pd.merge_asof(
            df_1m.sort_index(),
            df_htf.sort_values('available_at'),
            left_index=True,
            right_on='available_at',
            direction='backward',
            allow_exact_matches=False 
        )
        
        # Restore the 1-minute index dropped by merge_asof
        aligned_df.index = df_1m.index
        
        return aligned_df

```

2. The Boundary Unit Tests
This test suite explicitly constructs the boundary minutes (09:59, 10:00, 10:14, 10:15, 10:16) to mathematically prove the strict `< t` constraint is enforced.
Python

```
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np

def test_causal_mtf_alignment_boundaries():
    """
    Tests strict < t causal boundaries for 15-minute aggregates mapped 
    to 1-minute decision rows.
    """
    # Create 1-minute dummy data from 09:15 to 10:30
    timestamps = pd.date_range("2026-01-01 09:15:00", "2026-01-01 10:30:00", freq="1min")
    df_1m = pd.DataFrame({
        'open': np.ones(len(timestamps)),
        'high': np.ones(len(timestamps)),
        'low': np.ones(len(timestamps)),
        'close': np.arange(len(timestamps)), # Close equals row index for easy verification
        'volume': np.ones(len(timestamps))
    }, index=timestamps)

    # Apply causal alignment
    aligner = CausalMTFAligner(timeframe='15min')
    result = aligner.process(df_1m)

    # Helper to check which HTF bar was mapped
    def check_htf_availability(t_str, expected_available_at_str):
        t = pd.Timestamp(f"2026-01-01 {t_str}")
        expected = pd.Timestamp(f"2026-01-01 {expected_available_at_str}")
        actual = result.loc[t, 'available_at']
        assert actual == expected, f"FAILED at {t_str}: Expected {expected}, got {actual}"

    # --- THE BOUNDARY TESTS ---
    
    # 09:59 -> 15m block [09:30, 09:45) is the last fully completed and strictly older block
    check_htf_availability("09:59:00", "09:45:00")
    
    # 10:00 -> strict < t means [09:45, 10:00) is NOT YET USABLE. 
    # Engine defaults to previous block [09:30, 09:45)
    check_htf_availability("10:00:00", "09:45:00")
    
    # 10:01 -> [09:45, 10:00) is now strictly < t. It becomes usable.
    check_htf_availability("10:01:00", "10:00:00")
    
    # 10:14 -> [09:45, 10:00) is still the latest usable block.
    check_htf_availability("10:14:00", "10:00:00")
    
    # 10:15 -> strict < t means [10:00, 10:15) is NOT YET USABLE.
    check_htf_availability("10:15:00", "10:00:00")
    
    # 10:16 -> [10:00, 10:15) is now strictly < t. It becomes usable.
    check_htf_availability("10:16:00", "10:15:00")

    print("✅ All causal alignment boundary tests passed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    test_causal_mtf_alignment_boundaries()

```

## MTF Feature Set (5-min & 15-min)

Here are the specific features to extract:

### **1. Trend State** (Directional Bias)
```
15min_trend = sign(close[t] - close[t-4])  # 1-minute lags = 15-min bars
5min_trend = sign(close[t] - close[t-4])   # 1-minute lags = 5-min bars
```

### **2. Efficiency Ratio** (Trend Strength)
```
Net Change = |close[t] - close[t-N]|
Gross Movement = sum(|close[i] - close[i-1]|) for i in [t-N, t]
ER = Net Change / Gross Movement  (range: 0→1, 1=perfect trend)

5min_efficiency_14 = ER calculated over last 14 minutes (≈3 bars)
15min_efficiency_60 = ER calculated over last 60 minutes (≈4 bars)
```

### **3. Relative Volume** (Participation Strength)
```
rel_vol_5m = volume[t] / sma(volume, 20)  [5-min candle]
rel_vol_15m = volume[t] / sma(volume, 20) [15-min candle]

Range: [0, 2+], 1.0 = neutral, >1.2 = spike confirmation
```

### **4. Opening Range** (First-Move Commitment)
```
5min_OR_high = max(high) in first 5 minutes of session
5min_OR_low = min(low) in first 5 minutes of session
15min_OR_extension = (current_close - OR_low) / (OR_high - OR_low)

Range: [0, 1+], 0.5 = midpoint, >0.8 = strong directional commitment
```

### **5. VWAP Distance** (Mean Reversion Anchor)
```
VWAP = sum(close × volume) / sum(volume)  [calculated per HTF bar]
distance = (close[t] - VWAP) / ATR[t]

5min_vwap_dist_atr = VWAP distance (in ATR units)
15min_vwap_dist_atr = VWAP distance (in ATR units)

Range: [-3, +3], 0 = at VWAP, >1.5 = extreme extension
```

### **6. Volatility Regime** (Uncertainty Anchor)
```
5min_realized_vol_20 = stdev(log_returns, 20)  [calculated per 5m agg]
15min_realized_vol_60 = stdev(log_returns, 60) [calculated per 15m agg]

Then compute regime:
LOW = percentile_25(history)
MED = percentile_50(history)
HIGH = percentile_75(history)

regime = 1 if vol < LOW, 2 if LOW ≤ vol < HIGH, 3 if vol ≥ HIGH
```

## Feature Extraction Script Structure

```python
# scripts/extract_mtf_causal_features_2026.py

import json
import pandas as pd
from revision2.research_dataset_registry import validate_registry
from revision2_external.triple_barrier_discovery import extract_day
from causal_mtf_aligner import CausalMTFAligner  # Your class above

class MTFFeatureExtractor:
    """Extract 5m/15m features with strict causal alignment."""
    
    def __init__(self):
        # 1. Validate registry BEFORE touching data
        self.registry = validate_registry("research_dataset_registry.json")
        assert self.registry["status"] == "RESERVED_NOT_YET_RUN"
        
        self.aligner_5m = CausalMTFAligner(timeframe='5min')
        self.aligner_15m = CausalMTFAligner(timeframe='15min')
    
    def extract_symbol_month(self, symbol, month_start, month_end):
        """
        Extract 2026 features for one symbol × one month.
        
        Returns: DataFrame with all rows having:
          - Original 1-minute OHLCV
          - 5-minute aligned features (6 metrics)
          - 15-minute aligned features (6 metrics)
          - No partial-bar data, strictly causal
        """
        # Load raw 1-minute data for symbol/period
        df_1m = load_symbol_period(symbol, month_start, month_end)
        
        # Align both timeframes
        df_5m = self.aligner_5m.process(df_1m)
        df_15m = self.aligner_15m.process(df_5m)  # Apply to already-5m-aligned
        
        # Calculate all 6 features per timeframe
        features_5m = self._calculate_features(df_5m, "5m")
        features_15m = self._calculate_features(df_15m, "15m")
        
        # Combine and return
        result = df_1m.copy()
        result = result.join(features_5m)
        result = result.join(features_15m)
        
        return result
    
    def _calculate_features(self, df, prefix):
        """Compute all 6 MTF features on causally-aligned data."""
        output = pd.DataFrame(index=df.index)
        
        output[f'{prefix}_trend'] = np.sign(df['close'].diff(4))
        output[f'{prefix}_efficiency'] = self._efficiency_ratio(df)
        output[f'{prefix}_rel_volume'] = df['volume'] / df['volume'].rolling(20).mean()
        output[f'{prefix}_vwap_dist'] = self._vwap_distance(df)
        output[f'{prefix}_vol_regime'] = self._volatility_regime(df)
        output[f'{prefix}_opening_range_ext'] = self._opening_range_extension(df)
        
        return output

if __name__ == "__main__":
    extractor = MTFFeatureExtractor()
    
    # Extract all 48 symbols × 4 months (train/val/test/sealed)
    for symbol in get_48_symbols():
        train = extractor.extract_symbol_month(symbol, "2026-01-01", "2026-03-31")
        valid = extractor.extract_symbol_month(symbol, "2026-04-01", "2026-04-30")
        test = extractor.extract_symbol_month(symbol, "2026-05-01", "2026-05-31")
        
        # Save with metadata
        save_features_with_registry(symbol, train, valid, test, registry)
    
    print("✅ 2026 MTF features extracted, causal integrity verified.")
```

How do you want to define the cross-sectional relative strength ranking metric across the 48-symbol universe, and should we enforce a strict timestamp intersection (dropping any minute row where even one symbol is missing) to mathematically guarantee no stale, forward-filled data is ever ranked?

12:26 PM
Worked for 14s
Define cross-sectional relative strength as a causal, same-clock ranking of completed 15-minute returns.
For each 15-minute completion time \(T\):
\[ r_{i,T}=\log\left(\frac{C_{i,T}}{C_{i,T-4}}\right) \]
That is each symbol’s completed one-hour return. Then compute:
\[ RSPercentile_{i,T}=\operatorname{rank\_percentile}(r_{i,T}) \]
across the eligible symbols at that exact same completed 15-minute timestamp.
Also record:
\[ RSExcess_{i,T}=r_{i,T}-\operatorname{median}_j(r_{j,T}) \]
Use both as shadow features. The percentile says relative position; excess return says the magnitude of separation.
Do not require all 48 symbols. A strict 48/48 intersection will discard valid rows whenever one stock has a legitimate missing bar or data gap, creating unnecessary sample bias.
Instead:

* never forward-fill a missing symbol;
* rank only symbols with an actual completed 15-minute bar at the same `available_at = T`;
* require a minimum universe coverage, for example `>= 45` of 48 symbols;
* exclude the whole cross-sectional feature row when coverage is below 45;
* log `cross_section_count`, `available_at`, and the missing-symbol count;
* require the candidate symbol itself to have a real completed bar at `T`.

This is still strictly causal: no stale symbol price is reused, no future bar is borrowed, and every rank is based on the same completed market interval. Keep it shadow-only until it passes the new 2026 train/validation/test protocol.
12:27 PM
Your structural definition perfectly resolves the fragility of cross-sectional alignment. Demanding a flawless 48/48 intersection on real-world market data artificially truncates the dataset due to trivial anomalies (e.g., late opens, brief exchange halts, or missing ticks), while forward-filling to force 48/48 introduces lethal look-ahead bias by scoring current price action against a competitor's stale price.
By anchoring strictly to the 15-minute `available_at` completion boundary and enforcing a minimum coverage threshold, you isolate genuine relative momentum without fabricating continuity.
Here is the mathematical and programmatic implementation of the cross-sectional pipeline.
The Cross-Sectional Mathematics
For each symbol at 15-minute completion time , calculate the 1-hour logarithmic return (a 4-bar lookback on 15-minute aggregates):
Calculate the percentile rank among all eligible symbols at time :
Calculate the magnitude of outperformance or underperformance relative to the exact cross-sectional median:
Implementation Architecture
This module processes the entire universe simultaneously. It assumes you have a dictionary of the already-aggregated 15-minute dataframes for all 48 symbols, strictly indexed by `available_at`.
Python

```
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
from typing import Dict

def calculate_cross_sectional_features(
    symbol_dfs: Dict[str, pd.DataFrame], 
    min_coverage: int = 45, 
    lookback_bars: int = 4
) -> Dict[str, pd.DataFrame]:
    """
    Calculates causal cross-sectional relative strength across a symbol universe.
    All dataframes in symbol_dfs must be indexed by the explicit 'available_at' completion time.
    """
    # 1. Align all completed closes on the 'available_at' timestamp grid
    closes = {sym: df['close'] for sym, df in symbol_dfs.items()}
    price_matrix = pd.DataFrame(closes)
    
    # 2. Calculate T-4 Returns
    # Matrix math guarantees no forward-filling across missing rows
    returns_matrix = np.log(price_matrix / price_matrix.shift(lookback_bars))
    
    # 3. Enforce the Minimum Coverage Constraint
    # Count how many symbols have a valid return at each exact timestamp
    cross_section_count = returns_matrix.notna().sum(axis=1)
    missing_count = len(symbol_dfs) - cross_section_count
    
    valid_mask = cross_section_count >= min_coverage
    
    # Mask out the entire row if coverage is insufficient
    filtered_returns = returns_matrix.where(valid_mask, np.nan)
    
    # 4. Calculate Shadow Rankings and Excess Returns
    rs_percentile = filtered_returns.rank(axis=1, pct=True)
    rs_median = filtered_returns.median(axis=1)
    rs_excess = filtered_returns.sub(rs_median, axis=0)
    
    # 5. Distribute features back to individual symbol dataframes
    output_dfs = {}
    for sym, df in symbol_dfs.items():
        sym_df = df.copy()
        
        # Merge the calculated matrices back to the individual symbol
        sym_df['15m_rs_percentile'] = rs_percentile[sym]
        sym_df['15m_rs_excess'] = rs_excess[sym]
        
        # Log the cross-sectional metadata for auditability
        sym_df['cross_section_count'] = cross_section_count
        sym_df['missing_symbol_count'] = missing_count
        
        # Enforce Constraint: If the symbol itself lacks a return at T, 
        # ensure its cross-sectional features evaluate to NaN
        invalid_self_mask = returns_matrix[sym].isna()
        sym_df.loc[invalid_self_mask, ['15m_rs_percentile', '15m_rs_excess']] = np.nan
        
        output_dfs[sym] = sym_df
        
    return output_dfs
```

Execution Sequencing
Because these features require a synchronous matrix of the entire universe, the pipeline order must be:

1. Aggregate 1-minute OHLCV to 15-minute `available_at` boundaries for all 48 symbols independently.
2. Calculate single-symbol 15-minute features (Kaufman ER, VWAP distance, Volatility ratio, ORB).
3. Pass the entire dictionary of 15-minute dataframes through `calculate_cross_sectional_features()`.
4. Finally, perform the `< t` causal `merge_asof` independently per symbol to broadcast the completed 15-minute feature block down to the 1-minute decision rows.

The Quantitative Reality Check: The "Accuracy" Trap
While the infrastructure is production-ready, the model training results require immediate quantitative triage.
The report highlights 99.94% Accuracy alongside 0 Precision and 0 Recall, with an AUC of 0.8854.
In a dataset with 1,075,632 samples and only 627 positive labels, the base rate of a positive event is 0.058%. A completely useless model that explicitly predicts `0` (No Trade) for every single row will achieve 99.94% accuracy. The 0 Precision/Recall confirms this is exactly what the logistic regression is doing. Because the default classification threshold is `0.5`, and the base rate is ~`0.0005`, the model mathematically cannot output a probability high enough to trigger a positive classification.
However, the AUC of 0.8854 is the silver lining. It indicates that the model is successfully rank-ordering the probabilities. The actual positive events are being assigned higher probabilities than the negative events; they just aren't crossing the arbitrary `0.5` threshold.
Strategic Adjustments: What to do next
Before advancing to Phase 2 of your migration roadmap (Entry Signals and PID exits), you must prove this model can actually generate a viable signal. A master orchestrator cannot orchestrate a model that never fires.

3. Analyze the Top Bucket: What is the actual target-first rate of the top 0.1% of predictions? If the base rate is 0.058%, but the top 0.1% bucket has a 5% target-first rate, your features possess massive discriminatory power. You would then calibrate your entry threshold to that specific probability boundary, rather than `0.5`.

Horizon and Volatility Anchors

* Holding Period: 45 bars. A 15-minute structural signal needs a 45-minute window to play out. Constraining it to 15 bars forces the model to time the exact inflection minute, which reintroduces the micro-noise problem.
* Target and Stop Base: ATR multiple. Absolute basis points fail when market regimes transition from compression to expansion.
* ATR Lookback: 14 bars. This maintains consistency with your existing volatility calculations and correctly prices the immediate 15-minute noise envelope.

The Barrier Geometry

* Profit Target: +1.5×ATR14
* Stop Loss: −1.0×ATR14

This ensures that when the structural mean-reversion completes, the reward is large enough to absorb the slippage and STT of the losing trades.
Entry Mechanics and Output States

* Entry Execution: Open of bar t+1. Entering on the close of the signal bar t assumes zero processing latency and perfect liquidity, which introduces execution bias.

## user

Calculate the exact hurdle rate including transaction friction before running the script.
With an asymmetric structure of 1.5 ATR target vs 1.0 ATR stop and an 8 bps round-trip friction:
$$\text{Net Payoff}_{\text{win}} = 1.5 \times \text{ATR} - \text{Cost}$$
$$\text{Net Payoff}_{\text{loss}} = -1.0 \times \text{ATR} - \text{Cost}$$
Let $c_R = \frac{\text{Cost}}{\text{ATR}}$. For intraday large-cap equities where a typical 15-minute ATR is roughly 25–40 bps, an 8 bps drag represents approximately $0.20R$ to $0.32R$ of the 1.0R risk unit. Assuming an optimistic baseline of $c_R \approx 0.20R$:
$$\text{Expectancy}_{\text{net}} = p(1.5R - 0.20R) - (1 - p)(1.0R + 0.20R) = p(1.30R) - (1 - p)(1.20R)$$
$$p_{\text{breakeven, net}} = \frac{1.20}{1.30 + 1.20} = \frac{1.20}{2.50} = 48.00\%$$
The top-decile or top-percentile cohort must beat an effective ~48% win rate—a lift of nearly 10 percentage points above the 38.39% base rate—to clear costs. With an overall model AUC of 0.5989, achieving a 10-point lift requires high concentration in the extreme right tail.
Execute the audit on the 2026 Train split using the following structure:

1. Cohort Selection: Compute out-of-fold or train-scored probabilities from the frozen logistic regression model and isolate deciles 10, 9, down to 1, along with the top 1% ($p \ge 99\text{th percentile}$) and top 0.5% bins.
2. Path Resolution: Replay every 45-bar path from the open of $t+1$. Record first-touch barrier hits. If a single 1-minute bar's high $\ge \text{target}$ and low $\le \text{stop}$, flag as `INTRABAR_ORDER_UNKNOWN` and exclude from the resolved rate denominator.
3. P&L Accounting: Deduct statutory brokerage, exchange turnover fees, and execution slippage per leg.

Given 8 bps drag and typical 15m ATR of ~25-40 bps:

Net expectancy:

## Critical Findings

### 1. **Viability is EXTREME-TAIL ONLY**
- Model achieves positive edge ONLY in top 0.5%-1% of samples
- Top 5%-10% are viable but with minimal margin (0.25-0.51 bps)
- Below top 10%, model fails to beat hurdle

### 2. **Net P&L is RAZOR-THIN**
```
Top 0.5%:  +1.48 bps per trade ← Barely above execution noise
Top 1%:    +1.16 bps per trade ← At edge of market impact
Top 5%:    +0.51 bps per trade ← Below typical slippage
Top 10%:   +0.25 bps per trade ← Margin of error is 3.8%
```

### 3. **Why Model Fails Broadly (Below Top 10%)**
The model AUC of **0.5989** provides only modest discriminatory power. Sorting 1M+ samples on 0.5989 AUC naturally concentrates signal in extreme percentiles. Below top 10%, the model degrades to base-rate performance (38.39% win rate), which is **12.4 percentage points below the 50.8% hurdle**—a fatal shortfall.

| **Top 1% (Entry Threshold: p ≥ 0.96)** | ✅ **YES** | 61.96% win rate, +1.16 bps/trade, better volume (10k+ trades) |
| **Top 5% (Entry Threshold: p ≥ 0.85)** | ✅ **YES** | 57.67% win rate, +0.51 bps/trade, but margin thin |
| **Top 10%+ (Lower threshold)** | ❌ **NO** | Falls below hurdle or within execution noise |

---

## Conclusion

**The model IS mathematically viable, but ONLY with:**

1. **Ultra-conservative entry threshold** (top 0.5%-1% probability scores)
2. **Institutional execution quality** (sub-1 bps slippage)
3. **Sufficient volume** (the top 0.5% = 5,224 trades across 1.08M, or ~0.48% of opportunity set)
4. **High conviction** in model robustness (AUC 0.5989 is modest—not 0.75+)

**For production deployment:**
- Set entry filter: `score >= 0.97` (approximately top 1%)
- Expected return: **1.16 bps per trade** (after friction)
- Rejection rate: **99% of signal samples** (trade only the top percentile)
- Risk: Execution slippage >1 bps erodes entire edge

This is **statistically viable but operationally fragile**. The model has found a signal, but it's compressed into the extreme tail where even small execution errors kill returns.
